\\ELSIF{$\\mathcal{T} \\in \\{\\text{Collider, M-bias}\\}$}\n651\t        \\STATE $M \\gets \\text{MC-SIMEX}$ \\COMMENT{31.8\\% / 0.1\\% RMSE reduction}\n652\t    \\ELSE\n653\t        \\STATE $M \\gets \\emptyset$; flag: acquire gold labels \\COMMENT{no reliable gold-free method}\n654\t    \\ENDIF\n655\t\\ENDIF\n656\t\\STATE\n657\t\\STATE \\textbf{Step 3: FWL invariance warning}\n658\t\\IF{$\\mathcal{T} \\in \\{\\text{Conf., Med., Coll., M-bias}\\}$}\n659\t    \\STATE Skip Naive plug-in and MLA \\COMMENT{0\\% bias red.\\ on $T$ coeff.}\n660\t\\ENDIF\n661\t\\RETURN $(M, R)$\n662\t\\end{algorithmic}\n663\t\\end{algorithm}\n664\t\n665\t\\subsection{IV Wald Estimator: Diagonal-Dominance Subspaces}\n666\t\\label{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}\n667\t\n668\tSection~\\ref{sec:taxonomy_results} reports a 52.6\\% Wald sign-flip rate\n669\tover the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior. Because realistic LLM confusion\n670\tmatrices are diagonal-dominant, we replicate the exposure-DAG stratification\n671\tfor the IV topology, restricting the sample\n672\tspace to $\\{C : \\min_k C_{kk} \\geq d\\}$ for $d \\in \\{0, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9\\}$\n673\t(100{,}000 random matrices per threshold).\n674\t\n675\t\\begin{table}[h]\n676\t\\centering\n677\t\\caption{IV Wald estimator bias by minimum-diagonal subspace ($K{=}3$, $\\tau_{\\text{true}}{=}1.41$).\n678\tRates over 100{,}000 Dirichlet-truncated random $C$ per threshold.}\n679\t\\label{tab:iv_dirichlet_subspace}\n680\t\\small\n681\t\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n682\t\\toprule\n683\t\\textbf{Subspace} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & \\textbf{Worst} \\\\\n684\t\\midrule\n685\tFull           & 100.0\\% & 51.7\\% & 48.3\\% & $10^{5}\\!\\times$ \\\\\n686\t$d \\geq 0.5$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $28.6\\times$ \\\\\n687\t$d \\geq 0.7$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $2.4\\times$ \\\\\n688\t$d \\geq 0.9$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $1.2\\times$ \\\\\n689\t\\bottomrule\n690\t\\end{tabular}\n691\t\\end{table}\n692\t\n693\tThe contrast with exposure is instructive: at $d{\\geq}0.7$, exposure violation drops to 13.7\\% (sign reversal 2.1\\%, amplification 11.7\\%), while IV maintains 100\\% violation with pure amplification. IV sign reversal is entirely driven by pathological (near-random) confusion matrices where the Wald denominator crosses zero; diagonal dominance prevents this crossing but cannot prevent amplification. Even at $d{\\geq}0.9$, the Wald estimator inflates $|\\tau|$ by up to $1.24\\times$ with probability~1.\n694\t\n695\t\\subsection{Non-Differential Assumption Stress Test}\n696\t\\label{sec:nondiff_stress}\n697\t\n698\tOur main analysis assumes non-differential misclassification: the confusion matrix $C$ does not depend on the outcome $Y$. To assess sensitivity to violations of this assumption, we conduct a stress test on the exposure topology ($K{=}3$, $\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.7$, 10{,}000 random matrices per $\\varepsilon$). For each base matrix $C_0$, observations with $Y > \\text{median}(Y)$ are misclassified using a perturbed matrix $\\tilde{C} = \\text{renormalize}(C_0 + \\varepsilon \\cdot \\Delta)$ where $\\Delta_{jk} \\sim \\text{Uniform}(-1,1)$ for $j \\neq k$, while observations with $Y \\leq \\text{median}(Y)$ retain $C_0$.\n699\t\n700\t\\begin{table}[h]\n701\t\\centering\n702\t\\small\n703\t\\caption{Effect of differential misclassification on bias violation rates (exposure DAG, $K{=}3$, $\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.7$, $N{=}50{,}000$ MC samples per trial).}\n704\t\\label{tab:nondiff_stress}\n705\t\\begin{tabular}{cccc}\n706\t\\toprule\n707\t$\\varepsilon$ & Sign-Flip (\\%) & Amplification (\\%) & Any Violation (\\%) \\\\\n708\t\\midrule\n709\t0.00 & 2.0 & 11.5 & 13.3 \\\\\n710\t0.01 & 2.4 & 12.4 & 14.7 \\\\\n711\t0.05 & 2.8 & 13.0 & 15.7 \\\\\n712\t0.10 & 4.5 & 16.7 & 20.9 \\\\\n713\t\\bottomrule\n714\t\\end{tabular}\n715\t\\end{table}\n716\t\n717\tThe $\\varepsilon{=}0$ baseline (13.3\\% violation) closely matches the analytical result (13.7\\%) from Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy_results}. Even at $\\varepsilon{=}0.10$---a substantial departure from non-differential error---the violation rate increases by only 7.6 percentage points. Small departures ($\\varepsilon \\leq 0.05$) change rates by fewer than 3 percentage points, suggesting that our main results are moderately robust to mild violations of the non-differential assumption.\n718\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"77ae3a0e-e21c-4032-9b91-6b8a54da2af3","uuid":"e083878c-6fae-4f73-ae76-a9c84dad1271","timestamp":"2026-05-22T02:18:21.514Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod_0510/projects/causal_annot_error_attrib/docs/paper/appendix_proofs.tex","content":"\\section{Proof of Proposition~\\ref{prop:signflip}}\n\\label{app:proof}\n\nWe provide algebraic proofs of sign preservation for the confounding, collider, mediation, and front-door topologies, and an explicit counterexample for the exposure topology.\nThroughout, $A\\in\\{0,1,\\dots,K{-}1\\}$ with prior $p_k=P(A{=}k)$, and $C$ is a $K{\\times}K$ column-stochastic confusion matrix with $C_{jk}=P(A^*{=}j\\mid A{=}k)$.\nWe use category~0 as the reference and let $D_j=\\mathbf{1}(A{=}j)$, $D_j^*=\\mathbf{1}(A^*{=}j)$ for $j=1,\\dots,K{-}1$.\n\n\\subsection{Notation and Key Identities}\n\\label{app:notation}\n\nUnder non-differential misclassification, the misclassified moments factor linearly through $C$.\nDefine the population vectors:\n\\begin{align}\n  q_j &= P(A^*{=}j) = \\textstyle\\sum_k C_{jk}\\,p_k, \\label{eq:q}\\\\\n  \\mathrm{ct}_j &= \\mathbb{E}[T\\cdot D_j^*] = \\textstyle\\sum_k C_{jk}\\,\\mathbb{E}[T\\cdot D_k], \\label{eq:ct}\\\\\n  \\mathrm{cy}_j &= \\mathbb{E}[Y\\cdot D_j^*] = \\textstyle\\sum_k C_{jk}\\,\\mathbb{E}[Y\\cdot D_k]. \\label{eq:cy}\n\\end{align}\nWrite $q = Cp$, $\\mathrm{ct} = C\\,\\mathbb{E}[T\\cdot D]$, and $\\mathrm{cy} = C\\,\\mathbb{E}[Y\\cdot D]$, where $D=(D_0,\\dots,D_{K-1})^\\top$.\n\n\\subsection{Frisch--Waugh--Lovell Decomposition}\n\\label{app:fwl}\n\nFor topologies where $T$ is directly observed (confounding, mediation, collider, M-bias, front-door), the estimator regresses $Y$ on $Z^*=(1,T,D_1^*,\\dots,D_{K-1}^*)$.\nBy the Frisch--Waugh--Lovell (FWL) theorem, the coefficient on $T$ satisfies:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat{\\tau}(C)\n  = \\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}\\,\\tilde{Y}]}{\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]},\n  \\label{eq:fwl}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\tilde{T}=T-\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A^*]$ and $\\tilde{Y}=Y-\\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*]$ are residuals from projecting onto $(1,D_1^*,\\dots,D_{K-1}^*)$.\n\n\\begin{lemma}[Bias decomposition]\n\\label{lem:bias_decomp}\nSuppose $Y = \\tau T + f(A) + \\varepsilon_Y$ with $\\varepsilon_Y \\perp (T, A, A^*)$.\nThen\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C) = \\tau + \\frac{R(C)}{D(C)},\n  \\label{eq:bias_decomp}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere\n\\begin{align}\n  R(C) &= \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,f(A)\\mid A^*)\\bigr], \\label{eq:R}\\\\\n  D(C) &= \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)\\bigr] > 0. \\label{eq:D}\n\\end{align}\n\\end{lemma}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nExpand $\\tilde{Y}$ using $Y=\\tau T + f(A) + \\varepsilon_Y$:\n\\[\n  \\tilde{Y} = \\tau\\,\\tilde{T} + \\bigl(f(A) - \\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr) + \\varepsilon_Y.\n\\]\nSince $\\tilde{T}$ is measurable w.r.t.\\ $(T,A^*)$ and $\\varepsilon_Y\\perp(T,A,A^*)$:\n\\[\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}\\,\\tilde{Y}]\n  = \\tau\\,\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]\n  + \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\tilde{T}\\,\\bigl(f(A)-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr)\\bigr].\n\\]\nThe numerator residual is $R(C) = \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}(f(A)-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid A^*])]$, and the denominator is $D(C) = \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]$.\n\nBy the law of total covariance:\n\\[\n  R(C) = \\sum_{j=0}^{K-1} q_j\\,\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,f(A)\\mid A^*{=}j)\n  = \\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)].\n\\]\nFor $D(C)>0$: decompose $T = g(A) + \\varepsilon_T$ where $g(A)=\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A]$ (for confounding) or simply note that $T$ has exogenous variation independent of $A^*$ (for mediation).\nIn both cases, $\\tilde{T}=\\bigl(g(A)-\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr)+\\varepsilon_T'$, where $\\varepsilon_T'$ is the component of $T$ independent of $(A,A^*)$.\nHence $D(C)\\geq\\operatorname{Var}(\\varepsilon_T')>0$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\subsection{Confounding Topology ($A$ = Confounder)}\n\\label{app:confounding}\n\n\\paragraph{Data-generating process.}\n$A\\to T$, $A\\to Y$, $T\\to Y$.\nUsing dummy coding with category 0 as reference:\n\\begin{align*}\n  T &= \\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\\alpha_j D_j + \\varepsilon_T,\\quad\n  \\varepsilon_T\\sim\\mathcal{N}(0,\\sigma_T^2),\\;\\varepsilon_T\\perp A,\\\\\n  Y &= \\tau\\, T + \\sum_{j=1}^{K-1}\\beta_j^{(A)} D_j + \\varepsilon_Y,\\quad\n  \\varepsilon_Y\\sim\\mathcal{N}(0,1),\\;\\varepsilon_Y\\perp(T,A).\n\\end{align*}\nDefine $g(k)=\\sum_{j}\\alpha_j\\mathbf{1}(k{=}j)$ and $f(k)=\\sum_{j}\\beta_j^{(A)}\\mathbf{1}(k{=}j)$, so $g(0)=f(0)=0$.\n\n\\paragraph{Closed-form plim.}\nUsing the FWL decomposition, the coefficient on $T$ is:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C)\n  = \\frac{\\mu_{TY} - \\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\\frac{\\mathrm{ct}_j\\,\\mathrm{cy}_j}{q_j}}\n  {s_T - \\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\\frac{\\mathrm{ct}_j^2}{q_j}},\n  \\label{eq:plim_conf}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $s_T=\\mathbb{E}[T^2]$, $\\mu_{TY}=\\mathbb{E}[TY]$, and $\\mathrm{ct}_j$, $\\mathrm{cy}_j$, $q_j$ are defined in~\\eqref{eq:q}--\\eqref{eq:cy}.\n\n\\begin{proof}[Derivation]\nBy FWL, $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau = \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}Y]/\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]$ where $\\tilde{T}=T-\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A^*]$.\nSince $\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A^*{=}j]=\\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j$:\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}^2]\n  &= \\mathbb{E}[T^2] - \\sum_j q_j\\bigl(\\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j\\bigr)^2\n  = s_T - \\sum_j \\mathrm{ct}_j^2/q_j,\\\\\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}\\,Y]\n  &= \\mathbb{E}[TY] - \\sum_j \\bigl(\\mathrm{ct}_j/q_j\\bigr)\\,\\mathbb{E}[Y\\cdot D_j^*]\n  = \\mu_{TY} - \\sum_j \\mathrm{ct}_j\\,\\mathrm{cy}_j/q_j.\n\\end{align*}\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Verification at $C=I$.}\nWhen $C=I$: $q_j=p_j$, $\\mathrm{ct}_j=\\mathbb{E}[T D_j]=\\alpha_j p_j$, $\\mathrm{cy}_j=\\mathbb{E}[Y D_j]=p_j(\\tau\\alpha_j+\\beta_j^{(A)})$.\nDirect substitution yields numerator $= \\tau\\sigma_T^2$ and denominator $= \\sigma_T^2$, giving $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau = \\tau$.\n\n\\paragraph{Bias structure.}\nBy Lemma~\\ref{lem:bias_decomp}:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C) = \\tau + B(C),\\quad\n  B(C) = \\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),\\,f(A)\\mid A^*)]}{D(C)}.\n  \\label{eq:bias_conf}\n\\end{equation}\nBy the law of total covariance:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f\\mid A^*)]\n  = \\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))\n  - \\operatorname{Cov}\\bigl(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*],\\;\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr).\n  \\label{eq:lotc}\n\\end{equation}\n\nAt $C=I$: $\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*]=g(A)$, so the between-group covariance equals $\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)$ and $B(I)=0$.\n\nAt $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$ (uniform, $A^*\\perp A$): $\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*]$ is constant, so the between-group covariance is zero and\n\\begin{equation}\n  B_{\\mathrm{OV}} \\;\\equiv\\; B\\bigl(\\tfrac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top\\bigr)\n  = \\frac{\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),\\,f(A))}{\\operatorname{Var}(T)},\n  \\label{eq:bov}\n\\end{equation}\nwhich equals the classical omitted-variable bias (no control for $A$).\n\n\\paragraph{Sign preservation condition.}\nSince $D(C)>0$, sign$(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau) = $ sign$(\\tau\\cdot D(C) + R(C))$.\nFor sign preservation, it suffices that $R(C)$ does not overwhelm $\\tau\\cdot D(C)$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Confounding sign preservation --- proportional case]\n\\label{prop:conf_prop}\nIf $f(A) = \\lambda\\,g(A)$ for some constant $\\lambda$ (i.e., the confounder effects on $T$ and $Y$ are proportional), then\n\\begin{equation}\n  B(C) = \\bigl(1-\\eta(C)\\bigr)\\,B_{\\mathrm{OV}},\n  \\label{eq:convex}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere\n\\[\n  \\eta(C) = \\frac{\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*])\\;\\sigma_T^2}\n  {\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\bigl(\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))-\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*])+\\sigma_T^2\\bigr)}\n  \\;\\in\\;[0,1].\n\\]\nConsequently, $B(C)$ lies between $0$ and $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$, and $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ whenever $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau+B_{\\mathrm{OV}})$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWhen $f=\\lambda g$: the between-group covariance in~\\eqref{eq:lotc} becomes $\\lambda\\,\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])$, and $\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)=\\lambda\\,\\operatorname{Var}(g)$.\nSo $R(C)=\\lambda\\bigl(\\operatorname{Var}(g)-\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])\\bigr)=\\lambda\\,V_W$, where $V_W=\\operatorname{Var}(g)-V_B$ with $V_B=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])$.\nSimilarly, $D(C)=V_W+\\sigma_T^2$.\nHence\n\\[\n  B(C) = \\frac{\\lambda\\,V_W}{V_W+\\sigma_T^2},\\qquad\n  B_{\\mathrm{OV}} = \\frac{\\lambda\\,V}{V+\\sigma_T^2},\n\\]\nwhere $V=\\operatorname{Var}(g)=V_B+V_W$.\nThe ratio\n\\[\n  \\frac{B(C)}{B_{\\mathrm{OV}}} = \\frac{V_W(V+\\sigma_T^2)}{V(V_W+\\sigma_T^2)} = 1 - \\eta(C)\n\\]\nis a decreasing function of $V_B\\in[0,V]$.\nAt $V_B=0$ (no classification, $C$ uniform): $\\eta=0$, $B=B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$.\nAt $V_B=V$ (perfect classification, $C=I$): $\\eta=1$, $B=0$.\nSince $0\\leq V_B\\leq V$, we have $\\eta\\in[0,1]$ and $B(C)\\in[0,B_{\\mathrm{OV}}]$ (with the sign of $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$).\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Confounding sign preservation --- general case]\n\\label{prop:conf_gen}\nFor general $g$ and $f$ (not necessarily proportional), define the worst-case bias bound\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\bar{B} \\;=\\; \\frac{\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f(A))}}{\\sigma_T^2}.\n  \\label{eq:bbar}\n\\end{equation}\nIf $|\\tau| > \\bar{B}$, then $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C))=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ for \\emph{all} column-stochastic $C$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWe need $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau\\cdot D(C)+R(C))=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$, which holds if $|R(C)|<|\\tau|\\cdot D(C)$.\nSince $D(C)\\geq\\sigma_T^2$ (shown in Lemma~\\ref{lem:bias_decomp}), it suffices to show $|R(C)|<|\\tau|\\,\\sigma_T^2$.\n\n\\emph{Step 1: Bound $|R(C)|$.}\n$R(C)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A)\\mid A^*)]$.\nBy the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality applied to each within-group covariance:\n\\[\n  |\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f\\mid A^*{=}j)|\n  \\leq \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*{=}j)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*{=}j)}.\n\\]\nTaking expectations and applying Cauchy--Schwarz again:\n\\begin{align*}\n  |R(C)|\n  &\\leq \\mathbb{E}\\bigl[\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*)}\\bigr]\\\\\n  &\\leq \\sqrt{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(g\\mid A^*)]\\;\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(f\\mid A^*)]}\\\\\n  &\\leq \\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\;\\operatorname{Var}(f(A))},\n\\end{align*}\nwhere the last step uses $\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(X\\mid Y)]\\leq\\operatorname{Var}(X)$ (law of total variance).\n\n\\emph{Step 2: Conclude.}\nThe bias satisfies\n\\[\n  |B(C)| = \\frac{|R(C)|}{D(C)} \\leq \\frac{\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(f)}}{\\sigma_T^2} = \\bar{B}.\n\\]\nWhen $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}$, the bias cannot cancel $\\tau$, so $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ for all $C$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to the omitted-variable condition.}\nA weaker (necessary) condition for sign preservation is $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}})$ where $\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}}=\\tau+\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$.\nIf this fails, then $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$ (uniform noise) already flips the sign.\nThe rigorous sufficient condition $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}$ implies the weaker condition, since $|B_{\\mathrm{OV}}|=|\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)|/\\operatorname{Var}(T)\\leq\\bar{B}$ by Cauchy--Schwarz and $\\operatorname{Var}(T)\\geq\\sigma_T^2$.\n\n\\paragraph{Verification for paper parameters ($K{=}3$).}\nWith $\\tau{=}2.0$, $\\boldsymbol{\\alpha}{=}(1.5,{-}1.0)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}^{(A)}{=}(1.0,{-}0.5)$, $\\sigma_T{=}1.0$, $\\mathbf{p}{=}(0.40,0.35,0.25)$:\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\operatorname{Var}(g) &= \\sum_k p_k\\alpha_k^2 - \\bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_k p_k\\alpha_k\\bigr)^2 = 0.962,\\\\\n  \\operatorname{Var}(f) &= \\sum_k p_k(\\beta_k^{(A)})^2 - \\bigl(\\textstyle\\sum_k p_k\\beta_k^{(A)}\\bigr)^2 = 0.362,\\\\\n  \\bar{B} &= \\sqrt{0.962\\times 0.362}\\;/\\;1.0^2 = 0.590.\n\\end{align*}\nSince $|\\tau|{=}2.0 > \\bar{B}{=}0.590$, sign preservation is guaranteed for \\emph{all} column-stochastic $C$ by Proposition~\\ref{prop:conf_gen}.\nAdditionally, $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}=\\operatorname{Cov}(g,f)/\\operatorname{Var}(T)=0.588/1.962={+}0.300$, giving $\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}}{=}2.300>0$, confirming that both the necessary and sufficient conditions are satisfied.\n\n\\subsection{Mediation Topology ($A$ = Mediator)}\n\\label{app:mediation}\n\n\\paragraph{Data-generating process.}\n$T\\to A\\to Y$, $T\\to Y$.\n\\begin{align*}\n  T &\\sim \\operatorname{Bernoulli}(0.5),\\\\\n  A\\mid T &\\sim \\operatorname{Categorical}\\bigl(\\operatorname{softmax}(\\boldsymbol{\\gamma}+\\boldsymbol{\\delta}\\,T)\\bigr),\\\\\n  Y &= \\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}\\,T + f(A) + \\varepsilon_Y.\n\\end{align*}\n\n\\paragraph{Bias decomposition.}\nBy Lemma~\\ref{lem:bias_decomp}, the direct-effect estimator satisfies:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}(C) = \\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}} + \\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,f(A)\\mid A^*)]}{D(C)}.\n  \\label{eq:med_bias}\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe key structural difference from confounding: $T$ \\emph{causes} $A$, so the residual covariance $\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)$ captures the indirect effect of $T$ on $Y$ through $A$ that is not absorbed by conditioning on $A^*$.\n\n\\paragraph{Endpoint analysis.}\n\nAt $C=I$ ($A^*{=}A$): conditioning on $A$ blocks the mediated path $T\\to A\\to Y$, so $\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A)=0$ and $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau=\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}$.\n\nAt $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$ ($A^*\\perp A$): no information about $A$ is used, and\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)] &= \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)),\\\\\n  D(C) &= \\operatorname{Var}(T) = 0.25.\n\\end{align*}\nThe indirect effect is $\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}=\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}1]-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}0]$, and\n\\[\n  \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)) = 0.25\\,\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}.\n\\]\nHence at $C$ uniform: $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau = \\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}+\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}} = \\tau_{\\mathrm{total}}$.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical Finding (Mediation sign preservation).}\n\\label{find:med_sign}\n$\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})$ whenever $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$, where $\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}}=\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}+\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$.\nThis result is verified numerically for $100{,}000$ independently sampled column-stochastic matrices $C$; a closed-form proof for general $K$ remains open.\n\n\\begin{proof}\nLet $R(C)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)]$ and $D(C)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)]>0$.\n\n\\emph{Endpoints.}\nAt $C{=}I$: $f(A)$ is constant given $A{=}A^*$, so $R(I)=0$ and $B(I)=0$.\nAt $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$: $R=\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A))=0.25\\,\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$ and $D=\\operatorname{Var}(T)=0.25$, giving $B=\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$.\n\n\\emph{Within-group structure for binary $T$.}\nSince $T\\in\\{0,1\\}$, the within-$A^*$-group bias admits a revealing decomposition.\nLet $p_j=P(T{=}1\\mid A^*{=}j)$.\nThen $\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*{=}j)=p_j(1{-}p_j)$ and\n\\[\n  \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*{=}j)\n  = p_j(1{-}p_j)\\bigl[\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}1,A^*{=}j]-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}0,A^*{=}j]\\bigr].\n\\]\nDefine $\\delta_j=\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}1,A^*{=}j]-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}0,A^*{=}j]$ as the \\emph{conditional indirect effect} in the $A^*{=}j$ subpopulation.\nThen\n\\[\n  B(C)=\\frac{R(C)}{D(C)}\n  =\\frac{\\sum_j q_j\\,p_j(1{-}p_j)\\,\\delta_j}{\\sum_j q_j\\,p_j(1{-}p_j)}\n  =\\sum_j w_j\\,\\delta_j,\n\\]\na weighted average of the conditional indirect effects, with weights $w_j=q_j p_j(1{-}p_j)/\\sum_\\ell q_\\ell p_\\ell(1{-}p_\\ell)\\geq 0$ summing to 1.\n\nAt $C{=}I$: each $A^*{=}j$ group has $A{=}j$ a.s., so $\\delta_j=f(j)-f(j)=0$ and $B=0$.\nAt $C$ uniform: $\\delta_j=\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$ for all $j$ and $B=\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$.\n\n\\emph{Sign preservation.}\nWhen $\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),f(A))>0$ (the indirect effect reinforces the direct effect, i.e., $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}$ and $\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$ have the same sign), the bias $B(C)\\geq 0$ cannot oppose $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}$, so sign is preserved trivially.\n\nWhen $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}$ and $\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}$ have opposite signs but $|\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}|<|\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}|$ (i.e., $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$), sign reversal requires some $|\\delta_j|>|\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}|$ with the opposing sign.\nSince each $\\delta_j$ is a difference of weighted averages of $f$ values (with weights $P(A{=}k\\mid T{=}t,A^*{=}j)$), and $B(C)$ is a convex combination of $\\delta_j$, the bound $|B(C)|\\leq\\max_j|\\delta_j|$ holds.\nWe verify numerically that $\\max_j|\\delta_j|\\leq|\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}|$ for all 100{,}000 random $C$ matrices in our experiments, confirming sign preservation.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Verification for paper parameters ($K{=}3$).}\nWith $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}{=}0.5$, $\\boldsymbol{\\gamma}{=}(0,0,0)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\delta}{=}(0,0.8,{-}0.6)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}{=}(0,1.0,{-}0.5)$:\n\n$\\tau_{\\mathrm{ind}}=\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}1]-\\mathbb{E}[f(A)\\mid T{=}0]=0.351$ (computed from the softmax class probabilities).\n$\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}}=0.5+0.351=0.851>0$.\nSince $\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}}{>}0$ and $\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}}{>}0$, sign preservation holds for all $C$.\n\n\\subsection{Collider Topology ($A$ = Collider)}\n\\label{app:collider}\n\n\\paragraph{Data-generating process.}\n$T\\to A\\leftarrow Y$, $T\\to Y$.\nThe treatment effect is unconfounded:\n\\begin{align*}\n  T &\\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1),\\quad\n  Y = \\tau\\,T + \\varepsilon_Y,\\quad \\varepsilon_Y\\sim\\mathcal{N}(0,1),\\;\\varepsilon_Y\\perp T,\\\\\n  A\\mid T,Y &\\sim \\operatorname{Categorical}\\bigl(\\operatorname{softmax}(\\boldsymbol{\\delta}_T\\,T + \\boldsymbol{\\delta}_Y\\,Y)\\bigr).\n\\end{align*}\nThe collider $A$ is a common \\emph{effect} of $T$ and $Y$.\nThe no-control estimator (regressing $Y$ on $T$ alone) is consistent: $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau_{\\mathrm{nc}}=\\tau$.\nThe researcher erroneously controls for $A^*$, inducing Berkson's bias.\n\n\\paragraph{Bias decomposition.}\nUnlike confounding, $\\varepsilon_Y$ is not independent of $A$ (since $A$ depends on $Y=\\tau T+\\varepsilon_Y$), so Lemma~\\ref{lem:bias_decomp} does not apply directly.\nInstead, we derive the bias from the FWL identity~\\eqref{eq:fwl}.\nWriting $\\tilde{Y}=\\tau\\tilde{T}+(\\varepsilon_Y-\\mathbb{E}[\\varepsilon_Y\\mid A^*])$ and noting that $\\mathbb{E}[\\tilde{T}\\,\\varepsilon_Y]=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\varepsilon_Y\\mid A^*)]$ (since $T\\perp\\varepsilon_Y$ unconditionally):\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C) = \\tau + B(C),\\quad\n  B(C) = \\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,\\varepsilon_Y\\mid A^*)]}{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)]}.\n  \\label{eq:bias_col}\n\\end{equation}\nUnder non-differential misclassification ($A^*\\perp(T,\\varepsilon_Y)\\mid A$), define $g(k)=\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A{=}k]$ and $h(k)=\\mathbb{E}[\\varepsilon_Y\\mid A{=}k]$.\nBy the tower property, $\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A^*]=\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*]$ and $\\mathbb{E}[\\varepsilon_Y\\mid A^*]=\\mathbb{E}[h(A)\\mid A^*]$.\nSince $\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\varepsilon_Y)=0$, the law of total covariance gives:\n\\[\n  B(C) = \\frac{-\\operatorname{Cov}\\bigl(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*],\\;\\mathbb{E}[h(A)\\mid A^*]\\bigr)}{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)]}.\n\\]\n\n\\paragraph{Endpoint analysis.}\nAt $C=I$ ($A^*{=}A$):\n$B(I) = -\\operatorname{Cov}(g(A),h(A))\\,/\\,\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A)] = B_{\\mathrm{Berkson}}$,\nthe full Berkson's bias from conditioning on the collider.\n\nAt $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$ ($A^*\\perp A$):\n$\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*]$ and $\\mathbb{E}[h(A)\\mid A^*]$ are constant, so $B=0$ and $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau=\\tau$.\n\nThis is the structural reverse of confounding: misclassification attenuates the collider-induced bias toward zero, rather than growing bias from zero toward $B_{\\mathrm{OV}}$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Collider sign preservation --- proportional case]\n\\label{prop:col_prop}\nIf $h(A)=\\lambda\\,g(A)$ for some constant $\\lambda$, then $|B(C)|\\leq|B(I)|$ for all column-stochastic $C$, and $B(C)$ has the same sign as $B(I)$.\nConsequently, $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ whenever $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau+B_{\\mathrm{Berkson}})$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\nWhen $h=\\lambda g$, the numerator becomes $-\\lambda\\,\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*])$ and the denominator is $\\operatorname{Var}(T)-\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*])$.\nLet $V^*=\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g(A)\\mid A^*])\\in[0,V_g]$ where $V_g=\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))$.\nThen\n\\[\n  |B(C)| = |\\lambda|\\,\\frac{V^*}{\\operatorname{Var}(T)-V^*}.\n\\]\nThe function $x\\mapsto x/(a-x)$ is increasing on $[0,a)$.\nWith $a=\\operatorname{Var}(T)>V_g\\geq V^*$:\n$|B(C)|\\leq|\\lambda|\\,V_g/(\\operatorname{Var}(T)-V_g)=|B(I)|$.\nAt $V^*{=}0$ (uniform $C$): $B{=}0$.  At $V^*{=}V_g$ ($C{=}I$): $B{=}B(I)$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Collider sign preservation --- general case]\n\\label{prop:col_gen}\nDefine\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\bar{B}_{\\mathrm{col}} = \\frac{\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))\\,\\operatorname{Var}(h(A))}}{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A)]}.\n  \\label{eq:bbar_col}\n\\end{equation}\nIf $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}_{\\mathrm{col}}$, then $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C))=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)$ for all column-stochastic $C$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}\n\\emph{Step~1: Bound the numerator.}\nBy Cauchy--Schwarz:\n$|\\operatorname{Cov}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*],\\mathbb{E}[h\\mid A^*])|\\leq\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])\\,\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[h\\mid A^*])}$.\nSince conditional expectation reduces variance: $\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[g\\mid A^*])\\leq\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))$ and likewise for $h$.\n\n\\emph{Step~2: Bound the denominator.}\n$\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)]=\\operatorname{Var}(T)-\\operatorname{Var}(\\mathbb{E}[T\\mid A^*])\\geq\\operatorname{Var}(T)-\\operatorname{Var}(g(A))=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A)]$.\n\n\\emph{Step~3: Conclude.}\n$|B(C)|\\leq\\sqrt{\\operatorname{Var}(g)\\,\\operatorname{Var}(h)}\\,/\\,\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A)]=\\bar{B}_{\\mathrm{col}}$.\nWhen $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}_{\\mathrm{col}}$, the bias cannot overwhelm $\\tau$.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\paragraph{Verification for paper parameters ($K{=}3$).}\nWith $\\tau{=}1.0$, $\\boldsymbol{\\delta}_T{=}(0,\\,0.5,\\,{-}0.3)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\delta}_Y{=}(0,\\,0.8,\\,0.6)$, from $5{\\times}10^6$-sample Monte Carlo:\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\operatorname{Var}(g(A)) &= 0.187,\\quad\n  \\operatorname{Var}(h(A)) = 0.076,\\quad\n  \\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A)] = 0.813,\\\\\n  \\bar{B}_{\\mathrm{col}} &= \\sqrt{0.187\\times 0.076}\\;/\\;0.813 = 0.147.\n\\end{align*}\nSince $|\\tau|{=}1.0\\gg\\bar{B}_{\\mathrm{col}}{=}0.147$, Proposition~\\ref{prop:col_gen} guarantees sign preservation.\nAdditionally, $B_{\\mathrm{Berkson}}{=}{-}0.114$, giving $\\tau+B_{\\mathrm{Berkson}}{=}0.886{>}0$.\nVerified across $100{,}000$ random column-stochastic $C$: $\\tau_{\\mathrm{sign\\,flip}}{=}0\\%$, and $|B(C)|\\leq|B_{\\mathrm{Berkson}}|$ in all cases.\n\n\\subsection{Front-door Topology ($A$ = Mediator under Confounding)}\n\\label{app:frontdoor}\n\n\\paragraph{Data-generating process.}\n$T\\to A\\to Y$, $U\\to T$, $U\\to Y$.\nAn unobserved confounder $U$ affects both treatment and outcome:\n\\begin{align*}\n  U &\\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1),\\quad\n  T = \\alpha_U\\,U + \\eta,\\quad \\eta\\sim\\mathcal{N}(0,1),\\;\\eta\\perp U,\\\\\n  A\\mid T &\\sim \\operatorname{Categorical}\\bigl(\\operatorname{softmax}(\\boldsymbol{\\gamma}+\\boldsymbol{\\delta}\\,T)\\bigr),\\\\\n  Y &= f(A) + \\lambda\\,U + \\varepsilon_Y,\\quad \\varepsilon_Y\\perp(T,A,U).\n\\end{align*}\nThere is no direct $T{\\to}Y$ edge; the causal effect of $T$ on $Y$ operates entirely through $A$.\nThe researcher controls for $A^*$ in $Y\\sim 1+T+D_1^*+\\cdots+D_{K-1}^*$.\n\n\\paragraph{Bias decomposition.}\nSince the direct effect is zero, the FWL coefficient on $T$ captures only bias:\n\\[\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C) = \\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,Y\\mid A^*)]}{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)]}.\n\\]\nSubstituting $Y=f(A)+\\lambda U+\\varepsilon_Y$ and using $\\varepsilon_Y\\perp(T,A,A^*)$:\n\\begin{equation}\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C)\n  = \\underbrace{\\frac{\\lambda\\,\\alpha_U}{\\operatorname{Var}(T)}}_{\\text{confounding (constant)}}\n  \\;+\\; \\underbrace{\\frac{\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,\\,f(A)\\mid A^*)]}{D(C)}}_{\\displaystyle B_{\\mathrm{med}}(C)},\n  \\label{eq:fd_decomp}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $D(C)=\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*)]$.\n\n\\begin{proof}[Derivation of~\\eqref{eq:fd_decomp}]\nExpand the numerator as\n$\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A)\\mid A^*)] + \\lambda\\,\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,U\\mid A^*)]$.\nFor the confounding term: since $T=\\alpha_U U+\\eta$ with $(U,\\eta)$ jointly Gaussian, the regression residual $\\xi=U-\\frac{\\alpha_U}{\\operatorname{Var}(T)}T$ satisfies $\\xi\\perp T$.\nBecause $A$ is a function of $T$ (plus independent softmax noise) and $A^*$ is a function of $A$ (plus independent misclassification noise), $\\xi\\perp(T,A,A^*)$.\nTherefore\n\\[\n  \\operatorname{Cov}(T,U\\mid A^*{=}j)\n  = \\frac{\\alpha_U}{\\operatorname{Var}(T)}\\,\\operatorname{Var}(T\\mid A^*{=}j)\n  \\quad\\forall\\;j,\n\\]\nand $\\lambda\\,\\mathbb{E}[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,U\\mid A^*)]=\\frac{\\lambda\\alpha_U}{\\operatorname{Var}(T)}\\,D(C)$, yielding the constant first term after division by $D(C)$.\n\\end{proof}\n\nThis decomposition reveals that \\emph{misclassification does not affect the confounding component}.\nThe confusion matrix $C$ modulates only the mediation-type leakage $B_{\\mathrm{med}}(C)$, which has the same structure as in~§\\ref{app:mediation}.\n\n\\paragraph{Endpoint analysis.}\nAt $C=I$: $B_{\\mathrm{med}}(I)=0$ (controlling $A$ blocks the $T\\to A\\to Y$ path), and $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(I)=\\lambda\\alpha_U/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$ (pure confounding residual).\n\nAt $C=\\frac{1}{K}\\mathbf{1}\\mathbf{1}^\\top$: $B_{\\mathrm{med}}=\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A))/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$, and $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau_{\\mathrm{nc}}=[\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A))+\\lambda\\alpha_U]/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$, the naive regression coefficient with no controls.\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical Finding (Front-door sign preservation).}\n\\label{find:fd_sign}\nSince $B_{\\mathrm{med}}(C)$ has the same structure as the mediation bias (§\\ref{app:mediation})---interpolating between $0$ at $C{=}I$ and $\\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A))/\\operatorname{Var}(T)$ at $C$ uniform---sign preservation holds whenever the two endpoints have the same sign:\n\\[\n  \\operatorname{sign}\\!\\bigl(\\lambda\\alpha_U\\bigr)\n  = \\operatorname{sign}\\!\\bigl(\\lambda\\alpha_U + \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A))\\bigr).\n\\]\nThis result is verified numerically for $100{,}000$ independently sampled column-stochastic matrices $C$.\n\n\\paragraph{Verification for paper parameters ($K{=}3$).}\nWith $\\alpha_U{=}1.0$, $\\lambda{=}1.0$, $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}{=}(0,1.0,{-}0.5)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\gamma}{=}(0,0.5,{-}0.3)$, $\\boldsymbol{\\delta}{=}(0,1.0,{-}0.8)$:\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\lambda\\alpha_U/\\operatorname{Var}(T) &= 1.0\\times 1.0\\,/\\,2.0 = 0.500,\\\\\n  \\operatorname{Cov}(T,f(A))/\\operatorname{Var}(T) &= 0.594\\,/\\,2.0 = 0.297.\n\\end{align*}\nBoth components are positive, so $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\tau(C)\\in[0.500,\\,0.797]>0$ for all $C$.\n\n\\subsection{Exposure Topology: Sign Reversal Counterexample}\n\\label{app:exposure}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Exposure sign reversal]\n\\label{prop:exp_counter}\nFor $K{=}3$ with $A$ as the treatment variable, there exist a marginal distribution $\\mathbf{p}$, treatment effects $\\boldsymbol{\\beta}$, and a column-stochastic $C$ with $C_{kk}>0.5$ for all $k$, such that $\\operatorname{sign}(\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\beta_1)\\neq\\operatorname{sign}(\\beta_1)$.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proof}[Construction]\nConsider the exposure DGP $Y = \\beta_1 D_1 + \\beta_2 D_2 + \\varepsilon_Y$ with:\n\\begin{itemize}\n  \\item Uniform prior: $\\mathbf{p}=(1/3,\\,1/3,\\,1/3)$.\n  \\item True effects: $\\beta_1=1$,\\; $\\beta_2=-10$.\n\\end{itemize}\nChoose the confusion matrix\n\\[\n  C = \\begin{pmatrix}\n    0.60 & 0.10 & 0.10\\\\\n    0.10 & 0.60 & 0.39\\\\\n    0.30 & 0.30 & 0.51\n  \\end{pmatrix},\\qquad\n  C_{kk}\\in\\{0.60,\\,0.60,\\,0.51\\},\\;\\text{all}>0.5.\n\\]\nColumn sums are $1$ (verifiable by inspection).\n\nThe OLS estimator regresses $Y$ on $(1,D_1^*,D_2^*)$.\nThe plim of $\\hat\\beta_j$ equals $\\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*{=}j]-\\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*{=}0]$, where\n\\[\n  \\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*{=}j] = \\sum_{k=0}^{2} P(A{=}k\\mid A^*{=}j)\\,\\beta_k\n  = \\sum_{k=1}^{2}\\frac{C_{jk}\\,p_k}{q_j}\\,\\beta_k.\n\\]\n\n\\emph{Marginal distribution of $A^*$:}\n\\begin{align*}\n  q_0 &= \\tfrac{1}{3}(0.60+0.10+0.10)=0.267,\\\\\n  q_1 &= \\tfrac{1}{3}(0.10+0.60+0.39)=0.363,\\\\\n  q_2 &= \\tfrac{1}{3}(0.30+0.30+0.51)=0.370.\n\\end{align*}\n\n\\emph{Conditional means:}\n\\begin{align*}\n  \\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*{=}0]\n  &= \\frac{0.10\\times\\tfrac{1}{3}\\times 1 + 0.10\\times\\tfrac{1}{3}\\times({-}10)}{0.267}\n  = \\frac{0.0333-0.3333}{0.267} = -1.125,\\\\[4pt]\n  \\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*{=}1]\n  &= \\frac{0.60\\times\\tfrac{1}{3}\\times 1 + 0.39\\times\\tfrac{1}{3}\\times({-}10)}{0.363}\n  = \\frac{0.200-1.300}{0.363} = -3.028,\\\\[4pt]\n  \\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*{=}2]\n  &= \\frac{0.30\\times\\tfrac{1}{3}\\times 1 + 0.51\\times\\tfrac{1}{3}\\times({-}10)}{0.370}\n  = \\frac{0.100-1.700}{0.370} = -4.324.\n\\end{align*}\n\n\\emph{Estimated coefficient:}\n\\[\n  \\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\beta_1 = \\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*{=}1]-\\mathbb{E}[Y\\mid A^*{=}0]\n  = -3.028-(-1.125) = \\mathbf{-1.903}.\n\\]\nThe true $\\beta_1=+1>0$ but $\\operatorname{plim}\\hat\\beta_1=-1.903<0$: \\textbf{sign reversal}.\n\n\\emph{Mechanism.}\nThe large off-diagonal entry $C_{12}=0.39$ (probability of classifying true category~2 as $A^*{=}1$) routes substantial probability mass from category~2 ($\\beta_2=-10$) into the $A^*{=}1$ bin.\nDespite $C_{11}=0.60>0.5$, the contamination from the strongly negative $\\beta_2$ overwhelms the positive $\\beta_1$, reversing the sign of the estimated effect for category~1.\nThis failure mode is structurally impossible at $K{=}2$: a $2{\\times}2$ column-stochastic matrix with $C_{kk}>0.5$ has the form $C=\\bigl(\\begin{smallmatrix}a&1{-}b\\\\1{-}a&b\\end{smallmatrix}\\bigr)$ with $a,b>0.5$, and the single coefficient $\\hat\\beta_1$ is a positive-weighted average of $\\beta_1$, preserving sign.\nFor $K{\\geq}3$, the additional degrees of freedom in $C$ permit cross-contamination between categories with opposite-sign effects.\n\\end{proof}\n\n\\subsection{Summary of Conditions}\n\\label{app:conditions}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Sign preservation condition} & \\textbf{Paper DGP} \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}})$ & $\\checkmark$ ($2.0$ vs $2.3$)\\\\\nMediation & $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{dir}})=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{total}})$ & $\\checkmark$ ($0.5$ vs $0.85$)\\\\\nCollider & $|\\tau|>\\bar{B}_{\\mathrm{col}}$ (Prop.~\\ref{prop:col_gen}) & $\\checkmark$ ($1.0$ vs $0.15$)\\\\\nM-bias & Bias is attenuating (toward $\\tau$) & $\\checkmark$ \\\\\nFront-door & $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau)=\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}})$ (§\\ref{app:frontdoor}) & $\\checkmark$ ($0.50$ vs $0.80$)\\\\\n\\midrule\nExposure & Not guaranteed for $K\\geq 3$ & Counterexample above \\\\\nIV & Not guaranteed (denominator instability) & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Sufficient conditions for sign preservation under non-differential misclassification.\nFor confounding, mediation, and front-door, the condition requires the true (or clean-control) effect and the no-control effect to have the same sign.\nFor the collider, the condition bounds the Berkson's bias magnitude relative to $|\\tau|$.\nAll conditions are verified for our DGP parameters.}\n\\label{tab:sign_conditions}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Structural argument for M-bias topology.}\n\\emph{M-bias:} The M-structure creates a backdoor path through two latent common causes.\nMisclassification of $A$ weakens the statistical association along this backdoor path, reducing the spurious correlation rather than amplifying it.\nThe bias therefore interpolates between zero (perfect classification) and the full backdoor bias (no classification information), preserving sign under the same sufficient condition as confounding.\n\n\\paragraph{Remark.}\nThe sign preservation conditions for confounding, mediation, collider, and front-door are conditions on the DGP parameters (the relative strength of the causal effect versus the confounding, indirect, or collider-induced bias), not on the confusion matrix $C$.\nOnce the condition is satisfied, sign preservation holds for \\emph{all} column-stochastic $C$ with $C_{kk}>0$---no diagonal-dominance restriction is needed.\nConversely, if the condition fails (i.e., the omitted-variable or total-effect estimate has the opposite sign from $\\tau$), then there exist confusion matrices that produce sign reversal.\nFor the DGP parameters used throughout our experiments, the conditions are comfortably satisfied.\nThe sign-alignment condition is violated when the confounder has opposing effects on treatment and outcome (e.g., $\\gamma_A > 0$ on $T$ but $\\gamma_A < 0$ on $Y$), producing $\\operatorname{sign}(\\tau) \\neq \\operatorname{sign}(\\tau_{\\mathrm{OV}})$. In applied settings, such opposing effects are uncommon when the analyst adjusts for $A$ precisely because they believe it confounds the $T$--$Y$ relationship in a consistent direction. When the condition fails, sign reversal becomes possible under sufficient misclassification, though this represents a pathological DGP where adjustment itself may be questionable.\n\n\\section{Supplementary Tables, Figures, and Algorithm}\n\\label{app:supplementary}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_binarization.pdf}\n\\caption{Binarization collapse $\\Delta$ across topologies. Collapsing $K{=}3$ annotations to binary introduces additional bias varying 154$\\times$ from M-bias (0.006) to IV (0.926).}\n\\label{fig:binarization}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig6_asv_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{ASV magnitude ranges across topologies for HumAID ($K{=}10$). Left: magnitude ASV ($\\delta^*$) range across 8 LLM configurations; lower values indicate greater fragility. ``Safe'' topologies never exceed the 10\\% bias threshold. Right: percentage of configurations flagged as ASV-unsafe.}\n\\label{fig:asv_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RMSE reduction (\\%) by correction method and DAG topology (mean across 6 error structures, $N_{\\text{sim}}{=}1{,}000$). Positive = reduced RMSE; negative = increased RMSE. Naive/MLA show 0.0\\% for FWL-invariant topologies because $C^{-1}$ cannot change the $T$ coefficient. \\textbf{Bold}: best method per topology; \\underline{underline}: best gold-free method.}\n\\label{tab:correction}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Topology} & \\textbf{Naive} & \\textbf{MLA} & \\textbf{DSL} & \\textbf{SIMEX} & \\textbf{PPI++} \\\\\n\\midrule\nConfounding & 0.0 & 0.0 & \\underline{48.0} & 35.1 & \\textbf{83.2} \\\\\nMediation & 0.0 & 0.0 & \\underline{52.9} & 39.1 & \\textbf{54.1} \\\\\nCollider & 0.0 & 0.0 & $-$92.6 & \\underline{31.8} & \\textbf{50.5} \\\\\nM-bias & 0.0 & 0.0 & $-$267 & \\textbf{\\underline{0.1}} & $-$88.0 \\\\\n\\midrule\nFront-door & $-$52.7 & $-$111.5 & \\underline{$-$29.0} & $-$62.4 & \\textbf{74.1} \\\\\nExposure & $-$44.8 & $-$89.0 & $-$59.5 & \\underline{$-$9.8} & \\textbf{75.5} \\\\\nIV & $-$40.4 & $-$58.7 & $-$206.1 & \\underline{59.4} & \\textbf{79.4} \\\\\n\\midrule\nGrand mean & $-$19.7 & $-$37.0 & $-$79.0 & 13.3 & \\textbf{47.0} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[h]\n\\caption{Correction method selection for LLM-annotated causal inference.}\n\\label{alg:correction}\n\\small\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE DAG topology $\\mathcal{T}$, gold-label availability $G \\in \\{\\textsc{yes}, \\textsc{no}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Recommended method $M$, risk level $R$\n\\STATE\n\\STATE \\textbf{Step 1: Assess risk level}\n\\IF{$\\mathcal{T} \\in \\{\\text{M-bias}\\}$}\n    \\STATE $R \\gets \\textsc{Low}$ \\COMMENT{$|\\text{bias}| < 0.04$ for all $C$}\n\\ELSIF{$\\mathcal{T} \\in \\{\\text{Conf., Med., Coll., Front-door}\\}$}\n    \\STATE $R \\gets \\textsc{Medium}$ \\COMMENT{bounded bias, no sign reversal}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $R \\gets \\textsc{High}$ \\COMMENT{Exposure, IV: sign reversal 83.4\\% / 52.6\\%}\n\\ENDIF\n\\STATE\n\\STATE \\textbf{Step 2: Select correction method}\n\\IF{$G = \\textsc{yes}$}\n    \\IF{$\\mathcal{T} = \\text{M-bias}$}\n        \\STATE $M \\gets \\textsc{None}$ \\COMMENT{PPI++ RMSE red.\\ $-$88.0\\%; no correction preferred}\n    \\ELSE\n        \\STATE $M \\gets \\text{PPI++}$ \\COMMENT{47.0\\% RMSE red., 96.8\\% coverage}\n    \\ENDIF\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE \\COMMENT{no gold labels}\n    \\IF{$\\mathcal{T} \\in \\{\\text{Confounding, Mediation}\\}$}\n        \\STATE $M \\gets \\text{DSL}$ \\COMMENT{48.0\\% / 52.9\\% RMSE reduction}\n    \\ELSIF{$\\mathcal{T} = \\text{IV}$}\n        \\STATE $M \\gets \\text{MC-SIMEX}$ \\COMMENT{59.4\\% RMSE reduction}\n    \\ELSIF{$\\mathcal{T} \\in \\{\\text{Collider, M-bias}\\}$}\n        \\STATE $M \\gets \\text{MC-SIMEX}$ \\COMMENT{31.8\\% / 0.1\\% RMSE reduction}\n    \\ELSE\n        \\STATE $M \\gets \\emptyset$; flag: acquire gold labels \\COMMENT{no reliable gold-free method}\n    \\ENDIF\n\\ENDIF\n\\STATE\n\\STATE \\textbf{Step 3: FWL invariance warning}\n\\IF{$\\mathcal{T} \\in \\{\\text{Conf., Med., Coll., M-bias}\\}$}\n    \\STATE Skip Naive plug-in and MLA \\COMMENT{0\\% bias red.\\ on $T$ coeff.}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $(M, R)$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n\\subsection{IV Wald Estimator: Diagonal-Dominance Subspaces}\n\\label{sec:iv_dirichlet_subspace}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:taxonomy_results} reports a 52.6\\% Wald sign-flip rate\nover the full Dirichlet$(1,1,1)$ prior. Because realistic LLM confusion\nmatrices are diagonal-dominant, we replicate the exposure-DAG stratification\nfor the IV topology, restricting the sample\nspace to $\\{C : \\min_k C_{kk} \\geq d\\}$ for $d \\in \\{0, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9\\}$\n(100{,}000 random matrices per threshold).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{IV Wald estimator bias by minimum-diagonal subspace ($K{=}3$, $\\tau_{\\text{true}}{=}1.41$).\nRates over 100{,}000 Dirichlet-truncated random $C$ per threshold.}\n\\label{tab:iv_dirichlet_subspace}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Subspace} & \\textbf{Violation} & \\textbf{Sign Flip} & \\textbf{Amplif.} & \\textbf{Worst} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFull           & 100.0\\% & 51.7\\% & 48.3\\% & $10^{5}\\!\\times$ \\\\\n$d \\geq 0.5$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $28.6\\times$ \\\\\n$d \\geq 0.7$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $2.4\\times$ \\\\\n$d \\geq 0.9$   & 100.0\\% & 0.0\\%  & 100.0\\% & $1.2\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe contrast with exposure is instructive: at $d{\\geq}0.7$, exposure violation drops to 13.7\\% (sign reversal 2.1\\%, amplification 11.7\\%), while IV maintains 100\\% violation with pure amplification. IV sign reversal is entirely driven by pathological (near-random) confusion matrices where the Wald denominator crosses zero; diagonal dominance prevents this crossing but cannot prevent amplification. Even at $d{\\geq}0.9$, the Wald estimator inflates $|\\tau|$ by up to $1.24\\times$ with probability~1.\n\n\\subsection{Non-Differential Assumption Stress Test}\n\\label{sec:nondiff_stress}\n\nOur main analysis assumes non-differential misclassification: the confusion matrix $C$ does not depend on the outcome $Y$. To assess sensitivity to violations of this assumption, we conduct a stress test on the exposure topology ($K{=}3$, $\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.7$, 10{,}000 random matrices per $\\varepsilon$). For each base matrix $C_0$, observations with $Y > \\text{median}(Y)$ are misclassified using a perturbed matrix $\\tilde{C} = \\text{renormalize}(C_0 + \\varepsilon \\cdot \\Delta)$ where $\\Delta_{jk} \\sim \\text{Uniform}(-1,1)$ for $j \\neq k$, while observations with $Y \\leq \\text{median}(Y)$ retain $C_0$.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Effect of differential misclassification on bias violation rates (exposure DAG, $K{=}3$, $\\min_k C_{kk} \\geq 0.7$, $N{=}50{,}000$ MC samples per trial).}\n\\label{tab:nondiff_stress}\n\\begin{tabular}{cccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\varepsilon$ & Sign-Flip (\\%) & Amplification (\\%) & Any Violation (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.00 & 2.0 & 11.5 & 13.3 \\\\\n0.01 & 2.4 & 12.4 & 14.7 \\\\\n0.05 & 2.8 & 13.0 & 15.7 \\\\\n0.10 & 4.5 & 16.7 & 20.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe $\\varepsilon{=}0$ baseline (13.3\\% violation) closely matches the analytical result (13.7\\%) from Section~\\ref{sec:taxonomy_results}. Even at $\\varepsilon{=}0.10$---a substantial departure from non-differential error---the violation rate increases by only 7.6 percentage points. Small departures ($\\varepsilon \\leq 0.05$) change rates by fewer than 3 percentage points, suggesting that our main results are moderately robust to mild violations of the non-differential assumption.\n","numLines":718,"startLine":1,"totalLines":718}}}
